========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They can show me whatever “reasons” they want, I know what I know. Everybody keeps acting like I’m imagining it, but I know for a fact my wife’s relative came into this house to get at me. I mean, my tools don’t move themselves. I’ve got a system. It’s just like at the fabrication shop, you know? We use these heavy pneumatic presses, and if your calipers aren't exactly where you left them on the bench, it throws off the whole shift. My old foreman, Gary, he used to scream if a single wrench was out of place. Gary was a good guy, though. He actually gave me my first set of socket wrenches back in ninety-two when I was just starting out. 

But anyway, yeah, it's not just the tools. My fishing plans don’t get spoiled by accident every weekend either. I was supposed to go down to the reservoir last Saturday. The bass are really biting this time of year, especially if you use those bright green spinnerbaits. I actually make my own lures sometimes, just out of little pieces of scrap metal I bring home from work. But then I go out to the garage and my tackle box is completely jammed shut and the outboard motor won't start? Please. After all these months, I can see plain as day he’s been trying to take over my place and push me out.

Voices? Like, hearing things? No, no, I don't hear anything like that. Just the normal sounds of the house, you know? The pipes clanking, the fridge humming. It's been about nine months. Yeah, going on nine months now, ever since my wife's relative moved into our home. He had some money trouble or something, so she told him he could take the spare room. Brought so much junk with him, too. Just boxes and boxes. I had to help him carry this heavy oak dresser up the stairs. Real solid wood, not that cheap particle board crap you get at IKEA. I can't stand that stuff. You drive one screw into it and the whole thing just crumbles like a stale cookie. 

If you're gonna build furniture, you should use real fasteners, maybe some dowels and wood glue, or at least a decent cam lock system. I actually built our kitchen table myself out of reclaimed maple. Welded the base out of two-inch square steel tubing, powder-coated it flat black. It's a beautiful piece of work. Weighs an absolute ton, though. You aren't sliding that thing across the linoleum without a buddy. My wife wanted to put a tablecloth over it, but I told her, why cover up the wood? Maple has such a nice, tight grain, it's a shame to hide it. We had to go to three different lumber yards just to find boards that weren't warped. 

But yeah, nine months. Right when he moved into the house, that's exactly when all this started.

He actually gave me my first set of socket wrenches back in ninety-two when I was just starting out. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, it's not just the tools. No, no, I don't hear anything like that. Just the normal sounds of the house, you know? The pipes clanking, the fridge humming. That fridge is getting pretty old, actually. It's one of those Kenmore ones from back in the early two thousands. They built things to last back then, not like the junk they sell now with all the computer chips in them. My neighbor, Frank, he bought one of those smart fridges last year, and the screen on the front went out after three months. Three months! He's got this riding mower, too, zero-turn radius, cost him a fortune... \n\nBut no, no voices. Just him sneaking around when I'm not looking. He's quiet about it, I'll give him that, but he's definitely coming in here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed your wife’s relative has been coming into your house to get at you?\n\nPatient: It's been about nine months. Yeah, going on nine months now, ever since my wife's relative moved into our home. He had some money trouble or something, so she told him he could take the spare room. Brought so much junk with him, too. Just boxes and boxes. I had to help him carry this heavy oak dresser up the stairs. Real solid wood, not that cheap particle board crap you get at IKEA. I can't stand that stuff. You drive one screw into it and the whole thing just crumbles like a stale cookie. \n\nIf you're gonna build furniture, you should use real fasteners, maybe some dowels and wood glue, or at least a decent cam lock system. I actually built our kitchen table myself out of reclaimed maple. Welded the base out of two-inch square steel tubing, powder-coated it flat black. It's a beautiful piece of work. Weighs an absolute ton, though. You aren't sliding that thing across the linoleum without a buddy. My wife wanted to put a tablecloth over it, but I told her, why cover up the wood? Maple has such a nice, tight grain, it's a shame to hide it. We had to go to three different lumber yards just to find boards that weren't warped. \n\nBut yeah, nine months. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hard to follow? No, nobody says that. I mean, my wife says I talk too much sometimes, but she's always saying stuff like that. She's got this habit of half-listening when she's watching her shows. You know those renovation shows on TV? Where they take a perfectly good house and just gut it for no reason? They always tear down load-bearing walls without even putting up proper temporary supports. It drives me crazy. I saw one episode where this guy, some designer with a scarf indoors, wanted to remove a whole structural column just to make the kitchen "flow" better into the living room. You can't just do that! The roof load has to go somewhere. You need a massive steel I-beam if you're gonna span that kind of distance, and even then, you gotta make sure the footings in the foundation can handle the point load. 

I actually helped pour a foundation for my brother-in-law's garage a few years back. Not the relative living with us, different guy. We ordered two trucks of ready-mix, 4000 PSI, and it was a hot day, so we had to work fast before it set up on us. We were screeding that concrete for hours. My back was killing me for a week. But we got it perfectly level. You drop a marble in the corner of that garage, it doesn't roll an inch. That's how you do a job right. 

So, no, I don't go off track. I just explain things thoroughly. People just don't have the attention span for details anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Caring less? No, I still care about things just fine. I care about my job, I care about doing things right. Like at the shop, I'm the only one who actually checks the tolerances on the sheet metal before we start bending it. You get a batch of 14-gauge steel from the supplier, and sometimes it's rolling in at, like, .083 inches instead of .074. If you don't adjust the brake press for that, your angles are gonna be all out of whack. I tried telling the new kid that last week. He just started, straight out of high school, nice enough kid, but he's got his face in his phone half the day. I told him, you gotta measure twice, bend once. It's not like woodworking where you can just sand it down if you mess up. Metal is unforgiving. 

I remember when I first started fabricating, back in the nineties. We didn't have these fancy CNC machines doing all the work for us. You had to lay everything out by hand with a scribe and a combination square. It took real skill. I made this custom exhaust manifold for my old Chevy truck using just a manual tube bender and a TIG welder. Took me three weekends, but the welds were like a stack of dimes, just beautiful. I sold that truck to a guy two towns over, and I still see it driving around sometimes. Sounds incredible. 

But yeah, no, I still care about things. I just want people to leave me alone, mostly. That relative of hers, though, he definitely doesn't care about personal boundaries, I'll tell you that.

He's got this riding mower, too, zero-turn radius, cost him a fortune... \n\nBut no, no voices. Just him sneaking around when I'm not looking. He's quiet about it, I'll give him that, but he's definitely coming in here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed your wife’s relative has been coming into your house to get at you?\n\nPatient: It's been about nine months. Yeah, going on nine months now, ever since my wife's relative moved into our home. He had some money trouble or something, so she told him he could take the spare room. Brought so much junk with him, too. Just boxes and boxes. I had to help him carry this heavy oak dresser up the stairs. Real solid wood, not that cheap particle board crap you get at IKEA. I can't stand that stuff. You drive one screw into it and the whole thing just crumbles like a stale cookie. \n\nIf you're gonna build furniture, you should use real fasteners, maybe some dowels and wood glue, or at least a decent cam lock system. I actually built our kitchen table myself out of reclaimed maple. Welded the base out of two-inch square steel tubing, powder-coated it flat black. It's a beautiful piece of work. Weighs an absolute ton, though. You aren't sliding that thing across the linoleum without a buddy. My wife wanted to put a tablecloth over it, but I told her, why cover up the wood? Maple has such a nice, tight grain, it's a shame to hide it. We had to go to three different lumber yards just to find boards that weren't warped. \n\nBut yeah, nine months. Right when he moved into the house, that's exactly when all this started.\n\nDoctor: Do people tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you go off track when you talk?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? No, nobody says that. I mean, my wife says I talk too much sometimes, but she's always saying stuff like that. She's got this habit of half-listening when she's watching her shows. You know those renovation shows on TV? Where they take a perfectly good house and just gut it for no reason? They always tear down load-bearing walls without even putting up proper temporary supports. It drives me crazy. I saw one episode where this guy, some designer with a scarf indoors, wanted to remove a whole structural column just to make the kitchen \"flow\" better into the living room. You can't just do that! The roof load has to go somewhere. You need a massive steel I-beam if you're gonna span that kind of distance, and even then, you gotta make sure the footings in the foundation can handle the point load. \n\nI actually helped pour a foundation for my brother-in-law's garage a few years back. Not the relative living with us, different guy. We ordered two trucks of ready-mix, 4000 PSI, and it was a hot day, so we had to work fast before it set up on us. We were screeding that concrete for hours. My back was killing me for a week. But we got it perfectly level. You drop a marble in the corner of that garage, it doesn't roll an inch. That's how you do a job right. \n\nSo, no, I don't go off track. I just explain things thoroughly. People just don't have the attention span for details anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself caring a lot less about things or people than you used to?\n\nPatient: Caring less? No, I still care about things just fine. I care about my job, I care about doing things right. Like at the shop, I'm the only one who actually checks the tolerances on the sheet metal before we start bending it. You get a batch of 14-gauge steel from the supplier, and sometimes it's rolling in at, like, .083 inches instead of .074. If you don't adjust the brake press for that, your angles are gonna be all out of whack. I tried telling the new kid that last week. He just started, straight out of high school, nice enough kid, but he's got his face in his phone half the day. I told him, you gotta measure twice, bend once. It's not like woodworking where you can just sand it down if you mess up. Metal is unforgiving. \n\nI remember when I first started fabricating, back in the nineties. We didn't have these fancy CNC machines doing all the work for us. You had to lay everything out by hand with a scribe and a combination square. It took real skill. I made this custom exhaust manifold for my old Chevy truck using just a manual tube bender and a TIG welder. Took me three weekends, but the welds were like a stack of dimes, just beautiful. I sold that truck to a guy two towns over, and I still see it driving around sometimes. Sounds incredible. \n\nBut yeah, no, I still care about things. I just want people to leave me alone, mostly. That relative of hers, though, he definitely doesn't care about personal boundaries, I'll tell you that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it's just him. Just her relative. Nobody else is bothering me. I mean, I don't really talk to a lot of people outside of work anyway, so who would even bother? Most people just mind their own business. Like my neighbor, Dave. Dave's a good guy. Keeps his lawn immaculate. He's got one of those zero-turn mowers, a Toro, I think. Real nice machine. Dual hydrostatic transmission, so you can spin on a dime. I was looking into getting one myself, but my yard isn't really big enough to justify the cost. Plus, I kind of like walking behind my old Honda mower. It's got that twin-blade system, cuts the grass into tiny little pieces so it mulches right back into the soil. You don't even have to bag it. 

I actually rebuilt the carburetor on that Honda last spring. Ethanol gas just destroys those small engines if you let it sit over the winter. Gums up the jets something awful. You gotta pull the bowl off, soak the whole thing in carb cleaner, and blow out the passages with compressed air. I've got a 30-gallon air compressor in the garage, a two-stage upright. Runs on 220 volts. I had to wire a dedicated circuit for it myself. Ran 10-gauge wire all the way from the main breaker panel through conduit. You don't want to mess around with electrical work, you gotta make sure your connections are tight and you're using the right size breaker, otherwise you're just asking for an electrical fire. 

But yeah, nobody else is spying on me or anything. It's just him sneaking around.

I can't stand that stuff. You drive one screw into it and the whole thing just crumbles like a stale cookie. \n\nIf you're gonna build furniture, you should use real fasteners, maybe some dowels and wood glue, or at least a decent cam lock system. I actually built our kitchen table myself out of reclaimed maple. Welded the base out of two-inch square steel tubing, powder-coated it flat black. It's a beautiful piece of work. Weighs an absolute ton, though. You aren't sliding that thing across the linoleum without a buddy. My wife wanted to put a tablecloth over it, but I told her, why cover up the wood? They always tear down load-bearing walls without even putting up proper temporary supports. It drives me crazy. I saw one episode where this guy, some designer with a scarf indoors, wanted to remove a whole structural column just to make the kitchen \"flow\" better into the living room. You can't just do that! The roof load has to go somewhere. You need a massive steel I-beam if you're gonna span that kind of distance, and even then, you gotta make sure the footings in the foundation can handle the point load. \n\nI actually helped pour a foundation for my brother-in-law's garage a few years back. Not the relative living with us, different guy. We ordered two trucks of ready-mix, 4000 PSI, and it was a hot day, so we had to work fast before it set up on us. We were screeding that concrete for hours. My back was killing me for a week. But we got it perfectly level. You drop a marble in the corner of that garage, it doesn't roll an inch. That's how you do a job right. \n\nSo, no, I don't go off track. I just explain things thoroughly. People just don't have the attention span for details anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself caring a lot less about things or people than you used to?\n\nPatient: Caring less? No, I still care about things just fine. I care about my job, I care about doing things right. Like at the shop, I'm the only one who actually checks the tolerances on the sheet metal before we start bending it. You get a batch of 14-gauge steel from the supplier, and sometimes it's rolling in at, like, .083 inches instead of .074. If you don't adjust the brake press for that, your angles are gonna be all out of whack. I tried telling the new kid that last week. He just started, straight out of high school, nice enough kid, but he's got his face in his phone half the day. I told him, you gotta measure twice, bend once. It's not like woodworking where you can just sand it down if you mess up. Metal is unforgiving. \n\nI remember when I first started fabricating, back in the nineties. We didn't have these fancy CNC machines doing all the work for us. You had to lay everything out by hand with a scribe and a combination square. It took real skill. I made this custom exhaust manifold for my old Chevy truck using just a manual tube bender and a TIG welder. Took me three weekends, but the welds were like a stack of dimes, just beautiful. I sold that truck to a guy two towns over, and I still see it driving around sometimes. Sounds incredible. \n\nBut yeah, no, I still care about things. I just want people to leave me alone, mostly. That relative of hers, though, he definitely doesn't care about personal boundaries, I'll tell you that.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your wife’s relative, do you believe anyone else is trying to harm you or spy on you?\n\nPatient: No, it's just him. Just her relative. Nobody else is bothering me. I mean, I don't really talk to a lot of people outside of work anyway, so who would even bother? Most people just mind their own business. Like my neighbor, Dave. Dave's a good guy. Keeps his lawn immaculate. He's got one of those zero-turn mowers, a Toro, I think. Real nice machine. Dual hydrostatic transmission, so you can spin on a dime. I was looking into getting one myself, but my yard isn't really big enough to justify the cost. Plus, I kind of like walking behind my old Honda mower. It's got that twin-blade system, cuts the grass into tiny little pieces so it mulches right back into the soil. You don't even have to bag it. \n\nI actually rebuilt the carburetor on that Honda last spring. Ethanol gas just destroys those small engines if you let it sit over the winter. Gums up the jets something awful. You gotta pull the bowl off, soak the whole thing in carb cleaner, and blow out the passages with compressed air. I've got a 30-gallon air compressor in the garage, a two-stage upright. Runs on 220 volts. I had to wire a dedicated circuit for it myself. Ran 10-gauge wire all the way from the main breaker panel through conduit. You don't want to mess around with electrical work, you gotta make sure your connections are tight and you're using the right size breaker, otherwise you're just asking for an electrical fire. \n\nBut yeah, nobody else is spying on me or anything. It's just him sneaking around.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your wife’s relative ever done something to you that would be impossible in real life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Impossible? Like, magic or something? No, no, nothing like that. He's just a regular guy, just... sneaky. Sneaky and disrespectful. It's all real-world stuff, you know? Like going through my things or moving my tools. Speaking of tools, I was looking for my 10-millimeter socket the other day. It's always the 10-millimeter that goes missing, isn't it? Every mechanic I know says the same thing. You buy a whole set, and within a week, the 10-millimeter is gone. I think I left it under the hood of my wife's sedan when I was changing the battery terminals. 

Those factory terminals are garbage, just stamped steel. They corrode so fast. I cut them off and crimped on some solid brass marine-grade terminals. Much better connection. You gotta use a proper hydraulic crimper for that, though. You can't just smash it with a hammer or use those cheap wire strippers. A bad crimp means high resistance, and high resistance means heat. Next thing you know, your alternator is working overtime trying to charge the battery, and you're stranded in a parking lot. 

I helped a guy jump his car at the grocery store last month. He had these cheap jumper cables, like 10-gauge wire at best. The insulation was melting right off them before the car even turned over. I told him, you need at least 4-gauge copper for a proper jump, especially on a V8. I keep a set of custom-made 2-gauge cables in my trunk. Made them myself with welding lead. They'll start a diesel tractor in the dead of winter. 

But no, he hasn't done anything impossible. He's just a nuisance, a very real nuisance.

But then I go out to the garage and my tackle box is completely jammed shut and the outboard motor won't start? Please. After all these months, I can see plain as day he’s been trying to take over my place and push me out.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences tended to happen when you were cutting down or stopping drinking alcohol?\n\nPatient: Drinking? No, no, I don't... I mean, I have a couple beers on the weekend, maybe, but I'm not some kind of drunk. I don't get the shakes or anything like that. I actually prefer a good root beer, to be honest. You know, the kind in the glass bottles? A&W used to make a really good one, but then they changed the recipe or something. It just doesn't taste the same anymore. Kind of like how they don't make cars like they used to. My first car was a '78 Chevy Nova. Man, that thing was a tank. You could hit a brick wall and the wall would come out worse. I did all the work on it myself, too. Rebuilt the carburetor, put in a new alternator... I miss that car. But yeah, no, this has nothing to do with drinking. It's him. He's just... he's everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any major medical illness or head injury around the time this started?\n\nPatient: Sick? No. Hit my head? No, nothing like that. I've got a thick skull anyway, but I always wear my hard hat on the floor. You have to. OSHA is always breathing down our necks about PPE these days. We had this one inspector come through a while back, guy with a clipboard who probably never held an angle grinder in his life, trying to tell us our welding curtains were two inches too high off the floor. I mean, come on. \n\nYou ever do any TIG welding? It's an art. It's all about puddle control. Especially with aluminum. Aluminum is tricky because it doesn't change color before it melts, it just drops right out on you if you aren't paying attention. You gotta really watch it. My buddy Dave, he was a master at it. Best welder I ever saw. He retired down to Florida a couple years ago, actually. Bought himself a little pontoon boat, just fishes for snapper all day... \n\nBut anyway, no, my brain is fine. No medical problems at all. I'm perfectly healthy. That's why I know I'm not making this up. My head is clear, and I know for a fact he's coming in here messing with my stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Like, hearing things? No, no, I don't hear anything like that. Just the normal sounds of the house, you know? The pipes clanking, the fridge humming. That fridge is getting pretty old, actually. It's one of those Kenmore ones from back in the early two thousands. They built things to last back then, not like the junk they sell now with all the computer chips in them. My neighbor, Frank, he bought one of those smart fridges last year, and the screen on the front went out after three months. Three months! You can't even get a glass of water out of the door without the thing freezing up on you. I told him he should have just bought a basic model, but he's always got to have the newest gadget. He's got this riding mower, too, zero-turn radius, cost him a fortune... \n\nBut no, no voices. Just him sneaking around when I'm not looking. He's quiet about it, I'll give him that, but he's definitely coming in here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed your wife’s relative has been coming into your house to get at you?\n\nPatient: It's been about nine months. Yeah, going on nine months now, ever since my wife's relative moved into our home. He had some money trouble or something, so she told him he could take the spare room. Brought so much junk with him, too. Just boxes and boxes. I had to help him carry this heavy oak dresser up the stairs. Real solid wood, not that cheap particle board crap you get at IKEA. I can't stand that stuff. You drive one screw into it and the whole thing just crumbles like a stale cookie. \n\nIf you're gonna build furniture, you should use real fasteners, maybe some dowels and wood glue, or at least a decent cam lock system. I actually built our kitchen table myself out of reclaimed maple. Welded the base out of two-inch square steel tubing, powder-coated it flat black. It's a beautiful piece of work. Weighs an absolute ton, though. You aren't sliding that thing across the linoleum without a buddy. My wife wanted to put a tablecloth over it, but I told her, why cover up the wood? Maple has such a nice, tight grain, it's a shame to hide it. We had to go to three different lumber yards just to find boards that weren't warped. \n\nBut yeah, nine months. Right when he moved into the house, that's exactly when all this started.\n\nDoctor: Do people tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you go off track when you talk?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? No, nobody says that. I mean, my wife says I talk too much sometimes, but she's always saying stuff like that. She's got this habit of half-listening when she's watching her shows. You know those renovation shows on TV? Where they take a perfectly good house and just gut it for no reason? They always tear down load-bearing walls without even putting up proper temporary supports. It drives me crazy. I saw one episode where this guy, some designer with a scarf indoors, wanted to remove a whole structural column just to make the kitchen \"flow\" better into the living room. You can't just do that! The roof load has to go somewhere. You need a massive steel I-beam if you're gonna span that kind of distance, and even then, you gotta make sure the footings in the foundation can handle the point load. \n\nI actually helped pour a foundation for my brother-in-law's garage a few years back. Not the relative living with us, different guy. We ordered two trucks of ready-mix, 4000 PSI, and it was a hot day, so we had to work fast before it set up on us. We were screeding that concrete for hours. My back was killing me for a week. But we got it perfectly level. You drop a marble in the corner of that garage, it doesn't roll an inch. That's how you do a job right. \n\nSo, no, I don't go off track. I just explain things thoroughly. People just don't have the attention span for details anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself caring a lot less about things or people than you used to?\n\nPatient: Caring less? No, I still care about things just fine. I care about my job, I care about doing things right. Like at the shop, I'm the only one who actually checks the tolerances on the sheet metal before we start bending it. You get a batch of 14-gauge steel from the supplier, and sometimes it's rolling in at, like, .083 inches instead of .074. If you don't adjust the brake press for that, your angles are gonna be all out of whack. I tried telling the new kid that last week. He just started, straight out of high school, nice enough kid, but he's got his face in his phone half the day. I told him, you gotta measure twice, bend once. It's not like woodworking where you can just sand it down if you mess up. Metal is unforgiving. \n\nI remember when I first started fabricating, back in the nineties. We didn't have these fancy CNC machines doing all the work for us. You had to lay everything out by hand with a scribe and a combination square. It took real skill. I made this custom exhaust manifold for my old Chevy truck using just a manual tube bender and a TIG welder. Took me three weekends, but the welds were like a stack of dimes, just beautiful. I sold that truck to a guy two towns over, and I still see it driving around sometimes. Sounds incredible. \n\nBut yeah, no, I still care about things. I just want people to leave me alone, mostly. That relative of hers, though, he definitely doesn't care about personal boundaries, I'll tell you that.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your wife’s relative, do you believe anyone else is trying to harm you or spy on you?\n\nPatient: No, it's just him. Just her relative. Nobody else is bothering me. I mean, I don't really talk to a lot of people outside of work anyway, so who would even bother? Most people just mind their own business. Like my neighbor, Dave. Dave's a good guy. Keeps his lawn immaculate. He's got one of those zero-turn mowers, a Toro, I think. Real nice machine. Dual hydrostatic transmission, so you can spin on a dime. I was looking into getting one myself, but my yard isn't really big enough to justify the cost. Plus, I kind of like walking behind my old Honda mower. It's got that twin-blade system, cuts the grass into tiny little pieces so it mulches right back into the soil. You don't even have to bag it. \n\nI actually rebuilt the carburetor on that Honda last spring. Ethanol gas just destroys those small engines if you let it sit over the winter. Gums up the jets something awful. You gotta pull the bowl off, soak the whole thing in carb cleaner, and blow out the passages with compressed air. I've got a 30-gallon air compressor in the garage, a two-stage upright. Runs on 220 volts. I had to wire a dedicated circuit for it myself. Ran 10-gauge wire all the way from the main breaker panel through conduit. You don't want to mess around with electrical work, you gotta make sure your connections are tight and you're using the right size breaker, otherwise you're just asking for an electrical fire. \n\nBut yeah, nobody else is spying on me or anything. It's just him sneaking around.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife’s relative ever done something to you that would be impossible in real life?\n\nPatient: Impossible? Like, magic or something? No, no, nothing like that. He's just a regular guy, just... sneaky. Sneaky and disrespectful. It's all real-world stuff, you know? Like going through my things or moving my tools. Speaking of tools, I was looking for my 10-millimeter socket the other day. It's always the 10-millimeter that goes missing, isn't it? Every mechanic I know says the same thing. You buy a whole set, and within a week, the 10-millimeter is gone. I think I left it under the hood of my wife's sedan when I was changing the battery terminals. \n\nThose factory terminals are garbage, just stamped steel. They corrode so fast. I cut them off and crimped on some solid brass marine-grade terminals. Much better connection. You gotta use a proper hydraulic crimper for that, though. You can't just smash it with a hammer or use those cheap wire strippers. A bad crimp means high resistance, and high resistance means heat. Next thing you know, your alternator is working overtime trying to charge the battery, and you're stranded in a parking lot. \n\nI helped a guy jump his car at the grocery store last month. He had these cheap jumper cables, like 10-gauge wire at best. The insulation was melting right off them before the car even turned over. I told him, you need at least 4-gauge copper for a proper jump, especially on a V8. I keep a set of custom-made 2-gauge cables in my trunk. Made them myself with welding lead. They'll start a diesel tractor in the dead of winter. \n\nBut no, he hasn't done anything impossible. He's just a nuisance, a very real nuisance.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Flat? No, nobody's ever told me that. I mean, my face is just my face, you know? I talk normal. 

But you know what actually *is* flat? A Grade AA granite surface plate. We have this massive one at the shop, weighs about eight hundred pounds. Sits on a special heavy-duty steel stand with leveling feet. It's calibrated to within, like, fifty millionths of an inch. You take a precision gauge block and slide it on there, and the two surfaces are so perfectly flat they just wring together. It feels like they're magnetized, but it's really just the air getting pushed out and atmospheric pressure holding them tight. It's incredible. 

I was trying to explain that to the new kid last week, how you use it to check if a machined part is warped. But like I said, he doesn't really appreciate the science of it. You really need a good dial test indicator to measure runout on a plate like that anyway. I've got a Starrett indicator in my toolbox, ruby tip, made right here in America. Cost me a pretty penny, but you absolutely cannot trust those cheap imported knockoffs. They'll lose calibration if you just look at them funny, and then your measurements are garbage for the rest of the day. You drop one of those cheap ones, you might as well throw it in the scrap bin. 

But yeah, no, my voice isn't flat. I just talk how I talk. Nobody's complained about it.

It’s just like at the fabrication shop, you know? We use these heavy pneumatic presses, and if your calipers aren't exactly where you left them on the bench, it throws off the whole shift. My old foreman, Gary, he used to scream if a single wrench was out of place. Gary was a good guy, though. He actually gave me my first set of socket wrenches back in ninety-two when I was just starting out. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, it's not just the tools. My fishing plans don’t get spoiled by accident every weekend either. I was supposed to go down to the reservoir last Saturday. The bass are really biting this time of year, especially if you use those bright green spinnerbaits. I actually make my own lures sometimes, just out of little pieces of scrap metal I bring home from work. But then I go out to the garage and my tackle box is completely jammed shut and the outboard motor won't start? Please. After all these months, I can see plain as day he’s been trying to take over my place and push me out.\n\nDoctor: Have these experiences tended to happen when you were cutting down or stopping drinking alcohol?\n\nPatient: Drinking? No, no, I don't... I mean, I have a couple beers on the weekend, maybe, but I'm not some kind of drunk. I don't get the shakes or anything like that. I actually prefer a good root beer, to be honest. You know, the kind in the glass bottles? A&W used to make a really good one, but then they changed the recipe or something. It just doesn't taste the same anymore. Kind of like how they don't make cars like they used to. My first car was a '78 Chevy Nova. Man, that thing was a tank. You could hit a brick wall and the wall would come out worse. I did all the work on it myself, too. Rebuilt the carburetor, put in a new alternator... I miss that car. But yeah, no, this has nothing to do with drinking. It's him. He's just... he's everywhere.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any major medical illness or head injury around the time this started?\n\nPatient: Sick? No. Hit my head? No, nothing like that. I've got a thick skull anyway, but I always wear my hard hat on the floor. You have to. OSHA is always breathing down our necks about PPE these days. We had this one inspector come through a while back, guy with a clipboard who probably never held an angle grinder in his life, trying to tell us our welding curtains were two inches too high off the floor. I mean, come on. \n\nYou ever do any TIG welding? It's an art. It's all about puddle control. Especially with aluminum. Aluminum is tricky because it doesn't change color before it melts, it just drops right out on you if you aren't paying attention. You gotta really watch it. My buddy Dave, he was a master at it. Best welder I ever saw. He retired down to Florida a couple years ago, actually. Bought himself a little pontoon boat, just fishes for snapper all day... \n\nBut anyway, no, my brain is fine. No medical problems at all. I'm perfectly healthy. That's why I know I'm not making this up. My head is clear, and I know for a fact he's coming in here messing with my stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Like, hearing things? No, no, I don't hear anything like that. Just the normal sounds of the house, you know? The pipes clanking, the fridge humming. That fridge is getting pretty old, actually. It's one of those Kenmore ones from back in the early two thousands. They built things to last back then, not like the junk they sell now with all the computer chips in them. My neighbor, Frank, he bought one of those smart fridges last year, and the screen on the front went out after three months. Three months! You can't even get a glass of water out of the door without the thing freezing up on you. I told him he should have just bought a basic model, but he's always got to have the newest gadget. He's got this riding mower, too, zero-turn radius, cost him a fortune... \n\nBut no, no voices. Just him sneaking around when I'm not looking. He's quiet about it, I'll give him that, but he's definitely coming in here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you believed your wife’s relative has been coming into your house to get at you?\n\nPatient: It's been about nine months. Yeah, going on nine months now, ever since my wife's relative moved into our home. He had some money trouble or something, so she told him he could take the spare room. Brought so much junk with him, too. Just boxes and boxes. I had to help him carry this heavy oak dresser up the stairs. Real solid wood, not that cheap particle board crap you get at IKEA. I can't stand that stuff. You drive one screw into it and the whole thing just crumbles like a stale cookie. \n\nIf you're gonna build furniture, you should use real fasteners, maybe some dowels and wood glue, or at least a decent cam lock system. I actually built our kitchen table myself out of reclaimed maple. Welded the base out of two-inch square steel tubing, powder-coated it flat black. It's a beautiful piece of work. Weighs an absolute ton, though. You aren't sliding that thing across the linoleum without a buddy. My wife wanted to put a tablecloth over it, but I told her, why cover up the wood? Maple has such a nice, tight grain, it's a shame to hide it. We had to go to three different lumber yards just to find boards that weren't warped. \n\nBut yeah, nine months. Right when he moved into the house, that's exactly when all this started.\n\nDoctor: Do people tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that you go off track when you talk?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? No, nobody says that. I mean, my wife says I talk too much sometimes, but she's always saying stuff like that. She's got this habit of half-listening when she's watching her shows. You know those renovation shows on TV? Where they take a perfectly good house and just gut it for no reason? They always tear down load-bearing walls without even putting up proper temporary supports. It drives me crazy. I saw one episode where this guy, some designer with a scarf indoors, wanted to remove a whole structural column just to make the kitchen \"flow\" better into the living room. You can't just do that! The roof load has to go somewhere. You need a massive steel I-beam if you're gonna span that kind of distance, and even then, you gotta make sure the footings in the foundation can handle the point load. \n\nI actually helped pour a foundation for my brother-in-law's garage a few years back. Not the relative living with us, different guy. We ordered two trucks of ready-mix, 4000 PSI, and it was a hot day, so we had to work fast before it set up on us. We were screeding that concrete for hours. My back was killing me for a week. But we got it perfectly level. You drop a marble in the corner of that garage, it doesn't roll an inch. That's how you do a job right. \n\nSo, no, I don't go off track. I just explain things thoroughly. People just don't have the attention span for details anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself caring a lot less about things or people than you used to?\n\nPatient: Caring less? No, I still care about things just fine. I care about my job, I care about doing things right. Like at the shop, I'm the only one who actually checks the tolerances on the sheet metal before we start bending it. You get a batch of 14-gauge steel from the supplier, and sometimes it's rolling in at, like, .083 inches instead of .074. If you don't adjust the brake press for that, your angles are gonna be all out of whack. I tried telling the new kid that last week. He just started, straight out of high school, nice enough kid, but he's got his face in his phone half the day. I told him, you gotta measure twice, bend once. It's not like woodworking where you can just sand it down if you mess up. Metal is unforgiving. \n\nI remember when I first started fabricating, back in the nineties. We didn't have these fancy CNC machines doing all the work for us. You had to lay everything out by hand with a scribe and a combination square. It took real skill. I made this custom exhaust manifold for my old Chevy truck using just a manual tube bender and a TIG welder. Took me three weekends, but the welds were like a stack of dimes, just beautiful. I sold that truck to a guy two towns over, and I still see it driving around sometimes. Sounds incredible. \n\nBut yeah, no, I still care about things. I just want people to leave me alone, mostly. That relative of hers, though, he definitely doesn't care about personal boundaries, I'll tell you that.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your wife’s relative, do you believe anyone else is trying to harm you or spy on you?\n\nPatient: No, it's just him. Just her relative. Nobody else is bothering me. I mean, I don't really talk to a lot of people outside of work anyway, so who would even bother? Most people just mind their own business. Like my neighbor, Dave. Dave's a good guy. Keeps his lawn immaculate. He's got one of those zero-turn mowers, a Toro, I think. Real nice machine. Dual hydrostatic transmission, so you can spin on a dime. I was looking into getting one myself, but my yard isn't really big enough to justify the cost. Plus, I kind of like walking behind my old Honda mower. It's got that twin-blade system, cuts the grass into tiny little pieces so it mulches right back into the soil. You don't even have to bag it. \n\nI actually rebuilt the carburetor on that Honda last spring. Ethanol gas just destroys those small engines if you let it sit over the winter. Gums up the jets something awful. You gotta pull the bowl off, soak the whole thing in carb cleaner, and blow out the passages with compressed air. I've got a 30-gallon air compressor in the garage, a two-stage upright. Runs on 220 volts. I had to wire a dedicated circuit for it myself. Ran 10-gauge wire all the way from the main breaker panel through conduit. You don't want to mess around with electrical work, you gotta make sure your connections are tight and you're using the right size breaker, otherwise you're just asking for an electrical fire. \n\nBut yeah, nobody else is spying on me or anything. It's just him sneaking around.\n\nDoctor: Has your wife’s relative ever done something to you that would be impossible in real life?\n\nPatient: Impossible? Like, magic or something? No, no, nothing like that. He's just a regular guy, just... sneaky. Sneaky and disrespectful. It's all real-world stuff, you know? Like going through my things or moving my tools. Speaking of tools, I was looking for my 10-millimeter socket the other day. It's always the 10-millimeter that goes missing, isn't it? Every mechanic I know says the same thing. You buy a whole set, and within a week, the 10-millimeter is gone. I think I left it under the hood of my wife's sedan when I was changing the battery terminals. \n\nThose factory terminals are garbage, just stamped steel. They corrode so fast. I cut them off and crimped on some solid brass marine-grade terminals. Much better connection. You gotta use a proper hydraulic crimper for that, though. You can't just smash it with a hammer or use those cheap wire strippers. A bad crimp means high resistance, and high resistance means heat. Next thing you know, your alternator is working overtime trying to charge the battery, and you're stranded in a parking lot. \n\nI helped a guy jump his car at the grocery store last month. He had these cheap jumper cables, like 10-gauge wire at best. The insulation was melting right off them before the car even turned over. I told him, you need at least 4-gauge copper for a proper jump, especially on a V8. I keep a set of custom-made 2-gauge cables in my trunk. Made them myself with welding lead. They'll start a diesel tractor in the dead of winter. \n\nBut no, he hasn't done anything impossible. He's just a nuisance, a very real nuisance.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your face seems less expressive or that your voice sounds flat?\n\nPatient: Flat? No, nobody's ever told me that. I mean, my face is just my face, you know? I talk normal. \n\nBut you know what actually *is* flat? A Grade AA granite surface plate. We have this massive one at the shop, weighs about eight hundred pounds. Sits on a special heavy-duty steel stand with leveling feet. It's calibrated to within, like, fifty millionths of an inch. You take a precision gauge block and slide it on there, and the two surfaces are so perfectly flat they just wring together. It feels like they're magnetized, but it's really just the air getting pushed out and atmospheric pressure holding them tight. It's incredible. \n\nI was trying to explain that to the new kid last week, how you use it to check if a machined part is warped. But like I said, he doesn't really appreciate the science of it. You really need a good dial test indicator to measure runout on a plate like that anyway. I've got a Starrett indicator in my toolbox, ruby tip, made right here in America. Cost me a pretty penny, but you absolutely cannot trust those cheap imported knockoffs. They'll lose calibration if you just look at them funny, and then your measurements are garbage for the rest of the day. You drop one of those cheap ones, you might as well throw it in the scrap bin. \n\nBut yeah, no, my voice isn't flat. I just talk how I talk. Nobody's complained about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Delusional Disorder. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S019\n  name: Delusions\n  description: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\n  manifestation: I’m here because everybody keeps acting like I’m imagining it, but I know my wife’s relative came into this house to get at me—my tools don’t move themselves, my fishing plans don’t get spoiled by accident every weekend, and after all these months I can see plain as day he’s been trying to take over my place and push me out.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Functioning is not markedly impaired apart from the impact of the delusion(s) or its ramifications\n  manifestation: I still get up, go to the plant, do my assembler work, keep the bills paid, and take care of myself like I always have. Speaking of tools, I was looking for my 10-millimeter socket the other day. It's always the 10-millimeter that goes missing, isn't it? Every mechanic I know says the same thing. You buy a whole set, and within a week, the 10-millimeter is gone. I think I left it under the hood of my wife's sedan when I was changing the battery terminals. \n\nThose factory terminals are garbage, just stamped steel. They corrode so fast. I cut them off and crimped on some solid brass marine-grade terminals. Much better connection. You gotta use a proper hydraulic crimper for that, though. You can't just smash it with a hammer or use those cheap wire strippers. A bad crimp means high resistance, and high resistance means heat. Next thing you know, your alternator is working overtime trying to charge the battery, and you're stranded in a parking lot. \n\nI helped a guy jump his car at the grocery store last month. He had these cheap jumper cables, like 10-gauge wire at best. The insulation was melting right off them before the car even turned over. I told him, you need at least 4-gauge copper for a proper jump, especially on a V8. I keep a set of custom-made 2-gauge cables in my trunk. Made them myself with welding lead. We have this massive one at the shop, weighs about eight hundred pounds. Sits on a special heavy-duty steel stand with leveling feet. It's calibrated to within, like, fifty millionths of an inch. You take a precision gauge block and slide it on there, and the two surfaces are so perfectly flat they just wring together. It feels like they're magnetized, but it's really just the air getting pushed out and atmospheric pressure holding them tight. It's incredible. \n\nI was trying to explain that to the new kid last week, how you use it to check if a machined part is warped. But like I said, he doesn't really appreciate the science of it. Cost me a pretty penny, but you absolutely cannot trust those cheap imported knockoffs. They'll lose calibration if you just look at them funny, and then your measurements are garbage for the rest of the day. You drop one of those cheap ones, you might as well throw it in the scrap bin. \n\nBut yeah, no, my voice isn't flat. I just talk how I talk. Nobody's complained about it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas anyone other than your wife ever said that you go off on tangents or are hard to follow when you talk?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hard to follow? No, I don't think so. I mean, my wife says I talk too much about work, but nobody else says I'm hard to follow. I explain things pretty clearly, I think. You just have to pay attention to the details. 

Details are everything, especially in fabrication. Like when you're TIG welding aluminum. A lot of guys can't do it because they don't pay attention to the details. They don't prep the metal right. You can't just take a wire brush to it and start welding. Aluminum has an oxide layer that melts at a way higher temperature than the base metal, so if you don't clean it with acetone and use a dedicated stainless steel brush—one you've *never* used on carbon steel, mind you—you're just going to push contaminants right into the weld pool. 

And you gotta use alternating current on the machine. AC is what actually breaks up that oxide layer while you're welding. The positive half of the cycle cleans the metal, and the negative half penetrates. Most modern inverter machines let you adjust the AC balance, so you can dial in exactly how much cleaning action versus penetration you want. I usually run mine at about 70 percent EN—electrode negative—but it depends on how thick the material is. 

I was welding up a custom intake manifold for a buddy's drag car a few years back. Beautiful piece of work. Used 6061-T6 aluminum tubing, mandrel bent. You gotta back-purge the tubes with argon when you weld them, otherwise you get this nasty sugaring on the inside of the weld, and that disrupts the airflow. And in a drag car, airflow is horsepower. He ended up shaving two-tenths off his quarter-mile time with that manifold. 

But yeah, nobody says I'm hard to follow. I just like to make sure people understand how things actually work.

You take a precision gauge block and slide it on there, and the two surfaces are so perfectly flat they just wring together. It feels like they're magnetized, but it's really just the air getting pushed out and atmospheric pressure holding them tight. It's incredible. \n\nI was trying to explain that to the new kid last week, how you use it to check if a machined part is warped. But like I said, he doesn't really appreciate the science of it. You really need a good dial test indicator to measure runout on a plate like that anyway. I've got a Starrett indicator in my toolbox, ruby tip, made right here in America. Cost me a pretty penny, but you absolutely cannot trust those cheap imported knockoffs. They'll lose calibration if you just look at them funny, and then your measurements are garbage for the rest of the day. You drop one of those cheap ones, you might as well throw it in the scrap bin. \n\nBut yeah, no, my voice isn't flat. I just talk how I talk. Nobody's complained about it.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone other than your wife ever said that you go off on tangents or are hard to follow when you talk?\n\nPatient: Hard to follow? No, I don't think so. I mean, my wife says I talk too much about work, but nobody else says I'm hard to follow. I explain things pretty clearly, I think. You just have to pay attention to the details. \n\nDetails are everything, especially in fabrication. Like when you're TIG welding aluminum. A lot of guys can't do it because they don't pay attention to the details. They don't prep the metal right. You can't just take a wire brush to it and start welding. Aluminum has an oxide layer that melts at a way higher temperature than the base metal, so if you don't clean it with acetone and use a dedicated stainless steel brush—one you've *never* used on carbon steel, mind you—you're just going to push contaminants right into the weld pool. \n\nAnd you gotta use alternating current on the machine. AC is what actually breaks up that oxide layer while you're welding. The positive half of the cycle cleans the metal, and the negative half penetrates. Most modern inverter machines let you adjust the AC balance, so you can dial in exactly how much cleaning action versus penetration you want. I usually run mine at about 70 percent EN—electrode negative—but it depends on how thick the material is. \n\nI was welding up a custom intake manifold for a buddy's drag car a few years back. Beautiful piece of work. Used 6061-T6 aluminum tubing, mandrel bent. You gotta back-purge the tubes with argon when you weld them, otherwise you get this nasty sugaring on the inside of the weld, and that disrupts the airflow. And in a drag car, airflow is horsepower. He ended up shaving two-tenths off his quarter-mile time with that manifold. \n\nBut yeah, nobody says I'm hard to follow. I just like to make sure people understand how things actually work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\n  \"key\": \"S019\",\n  \"name\": \"Delusions\",\n  \"description\": \"Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\",\n  \"reason\": \"The doctor is asking if my belief remains certain even when presented with contradictory evidence, which directly addresses the fixed nature of my delusions.\"\n}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. My old foreman, Gary, he used to scream if a single wrench was out of place. Gary was a good guy, though. Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.
